Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured
2026-09-18

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In this pool photograph distributed by the Russian state agency Sputnik, Russia's President Vladimir Putin gives a speech during a ceremony to present state awards in the defence industry sector at the Kremlin in Moscow on September 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia is just one day into three-day elections that will determine the makeup of the lower house of the country’s parliament, the State Duma. But the outcome of the vote has been all but guaranteed since before the first ballots were cast. The elections, the first parliamentary vote the country has held since its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, are widely expected to result in the Kremlin-aligned United Russia party maintaining its overwhelming dominance in the Duma. The party currently holds 310 of the 450 seats in the chamber, giving it a supermajority and the power to make major legislative decisions without the input of other parties. It needs to keep at least 300 seats to retain that supermajority. Four other political parties are also expected to be represented in the Duma following the election, all of which generally vote in line with United Russia and support the war in Ukraine. They are the Communist Party of the Russian Federation, the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, the A Just Russia party, and the New People party. Democracy watchdogs and foreign governments have denounced Russia’s elections as being neither free nor fair. Russian officials have rejected such claims in the past, saying that the country carries out its voting process in line with the law. But experts tell TIME that the Russian government exerts its influence in ways that all but predetermine the results of elections and ensure that President Vladimir Putin and United Russia maintain control. And this year’s vote, says David Szakonyi, an expert on Russia and election fraud who is professor at George Washington University, is “occurring in the most repressive political environment in Russia's post-Soviet history.” Here's what experts have to say about how the Kremlin ensures elections result in its desired outcome—and why it continues to hold them. The Kremlin uses multiple levers to influence elections on the government level, at the polls, and in daily civilian life, according to Szakonyi. “Without a doubt, the most powerful tool” in the Kremlin’s arsenal, Szakonyi says, is limiting which candidates appear on the ballot in the first place. In the case of the ongoing parliamentary vote, the one anti-war party that was registered by the Central Election Commission, the Yabloko party, was prohibited from participating in the election by Russia’s Supreme Court. The high court ordered that the party be removed from the ballot last month in response to a lawsuit from the pro-Kremlin Rodina party that accused the Yabloko party, which has garnered support from young Russians and those opposed to the war in Ukraine, of copyright infringement, receiving foreign funding, and extremism. Szakoni says that the Kremlin has the ability to “harness the judicial system to pass rulings” such as the Supreme Court’s decision against the Yabloko party, which it uses as a “legal veneer” that shields the influence of Putin’s government over the ballot. This screening of anti-war and other opposition parties, he says, comes months in advance of elections, creating a list of candidates that includes little or no meaningful opposition at all. The Kremlin has also labeled opposition figures who oppose the war as “foreign agents,” a designation that prevents them from holding public office. In other instances, opposition leaders and activists such as Alexei Navalny have been prosecuted and imprisoned. Still others have fled the country. Experts tell TIME that the Russian government has also exercised its influence more directly on vote counts to achieve its desired election results. “Historically,” Szakonyi says, Russian electoral officials have “intervened regularly to deliver votes to the ruling party and take them away from competitors.” In the past, this was done via “crude ballot box stuffing,” he says; now, he asserts it is also performed by altering the results of online votes. Online voting is used in more than a third of Russia’s regions, which Szakonyi asserts puts more power in the government’s hands since it eliminates the physical tally represented by paper ballots. “The way that the electronic votes are being counted is almost completely a black box for the rest of society,” he says. Regina Smyth, an expert on Russia and authoritarian regimes who is a professor at Indiana University, says that “falsification,” or the practice of altering the number of votes that are counted, occurs at precinct election commissions, of which there are over 90,000, all across Russia, that she says are “mostly staffed by loyalists, and they will simply report false numbers.” There is also fraud, Smyth alleges, in “mobile voting,” when officials deliver and pick up ballots from people in their homes who are, for example, disabled or ill and unable to vote otherwise. On a deeper level, an “implicit threat” stemming from the Russian government sways countless votes each election cycle, Szakonyi contends. “Many Russian voters do not have the autonomy to cast an independent vote. They work for the state,” he says. He argues that many university students, healthcare workers, and teachers, whose livelihoods depend on state-run organizations, belong to a “block of captive, vulnerable voters” who fear prosecution by the Kremlin more than they value the influence of their ballot. Voting is also taking place this year in Russian-controlled areas of Ukraine: in Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, and in the Luhansk, Donetsk, Kherson, and Zaporizhzhia regions. “In the occupied territories of Ukraine, where the elections are being held in these illegally occupied territories, soldiers, heavily armed soldiers, travel from house to house coercing people to vote,” Smyth tells TIME. On top of the various methods the Kremlin allegedly uses to achieve the voting results it wants, there are slim to no oversight mechanisms in place to document election interference in the country. Golos, which was the only independent election watchdog within Russia, shuttered in 2025 following years of governmental pressure after its chairman was found guilty of working with an "undesirable organization" and imprisoned in a penal colony. Szakonyi describes Golos’ loss as the “biggest blow toward any type of accountability” for the Kremlin, saying it was “the biggest tool that civil society had to identify, uncover, and publicize the fraud that was basically happening in every single electoral cycle.” Russia has also refused to invite international election monitors from the Organization for Security ​and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE), the world’s largest regional security organization, to observe this year’s elections. Maria Telalian, director of the OSCE’s election-monitoring office, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, said in a statement last month that the decision not to invite any OSCE monitors was “deeply regrettable, particularly as it continues a pattern of not implementing the commitments that every OSCE participating State has undertaken towards its own citizens and the international community.” The ongoing vote marks the third nationwide election in Russia to take place without OSCE observation. Russia previously imposed severe limits on OSCE election observation in 2021 that the organization said made monitoring that year’s parliamentary elections in the country impossible. It did not invite the group’s monitors to the 2024 presidential election at all. Despite the results of the parliamentary elections being all but determined, they carry a symbolic weight crucial to Putin’s legitimacy in the public eye and his desire to continue the war against Ukraine, experts tell TIME. “There's lots of reasons to have elections. First and foremost, the Russian population expects elections,” Szakonyi says. “Putin's legitimacy depends on his ability to win election votes to the presidency and have his ruling party win on a regular interval, and demonstrate that they're popular and that their policies are in demand from the citizenry.” During an address in which Putin urged Russians to vote days before the elections began, the leader said that “your choice and resolve will show once again that millions of people stand behind our fighters” and that “participating in the elections is our shared contribution to Russia’s victory.” “This is a way for him to theoretically show that the Russian population is united behind his war and wants to continue fighting, and that Russia has had this referendum and will continue to push harder,” Szakonyi says.
TIME
Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage
2026-09-18

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This article discusses the full season of Netflix’s Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story is about female rage and repression. That isn’t my personal interpretation of the latest installment of Ian Brennan and Ryan Murphy’s Netflix true-crime anthology. It’s the talking point that the show’s makers have been leading with, in publicity materials and in multiple interviews. These are fine themes for the first season of Monster with a female subject, even if they’re not exactly unique within a post-Big-Little-Lies, #MeToo-informed TV landscape where so many dramas aimed at women double as amplifiers for gendered frustrations. The well of misogyny isn’t in danger of running dry anytime soon. But if we must keep diving into it, each descent should at least leave us with some new insight or nuance. Lizzie Borden, now streaming, fails to clear even that bar. Perhaps the least historically accurate Monster yet—and that’s saying something—the season uses the infamous tale of a Fall River, Mass. woman who probably hacked her wealthy father and stepmother to death with an axe in 1892 as a conduit to just about every plague on womankind you can imagine, past, present, and perennial. Rape. Domestic violence. Pregnancy and childbirth. Housework. Menstrual maladies. Pressure to marry. The premium put on physical beauty. The perils of sex work. Confinement in the home and subjugation to male relatives. By tying together Lizzie’s story with the legends of supposed forerunner Elizabeth Báthory and descendent Aileen Wuornos, Brennan, who wrote every episode, suggests a simplistic kinship between all female killers. Rebecca Hall, left, and Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix There’s no use fretting over the extremely purposeful anachronisms and inaccuracies in this retelling, which plays as fast and loose with the Borden family as that classic nursery rhyme. With 26-year-old Ella Beatty starring as a dreamy, blank-screen Lizzie, who ages from 32 to her 60s by the end of the season, and Charlie Hunnam and Rebecca Hall cast as parents who were really a couple of decades the actors’ seniors, everyone is much younger and prettier than their real-life counterparts. And more shocking, in some ways, than the hyperspeed double murder reenactment that opens the premiere, is what immediately follows it: a flashback to four months earlier, when Lizzie scares off a suitor by passing a bucket of menstrual blood on the white sofa where they’re sitting. As it turns out, what the fictionalized Bordens refer to as her “lady time” had some relevance to the actual case. But Lizzie Borden is such a massive remix, mingling facts with half-truths and rumors and fabrications, it barely matters which detail is which. Because its source material is not especially extensive—one double homicide, as opposed to the lengthy and lurid sagas of most past Monster subjects—the show is forced to stretch. The series is padded with Fall River horror scenes that either never happened (the oyster poisoning that leads to so much operatic barfing) or didn’t transpire remotely the way they’re shown (the murder of Bertha Manchester, played by Linda Reiter). Brennan also adds queer love to Lizzie’s life, in the form of the family’s maid, Bridget Sullivan, who they really did call Maggie, after a former servant. He is far from the first to investigate this angle; the 2018 film Lizzie, to take just one example, is also built around a romance, between Chloë Sevigny’s Lizzie and Kristen Stewart’s Bridget. Monster is, however, the first dramatization to make such imaginative use of the character. Phantom Thread breakout Vicky Krieps, who played ‘Bitch of Buchenwald’ Ilse Koch in Monster’s Ed Gein season, is a conspicuously Germanic Irish maid, with an ahistorical shag haircut dyed a series of shades reminiscent of, by turns, Wuornos and Ziggy Stardust. She has picked up skills in everything from cooking and cleaning to pharmacology in the course of her global travels. It’s no mystery why the awkward, sheltered Lizzie, who craves a bigger life, is enchanted by this glamorous, androgynous outsider. Maggie is easily the best part of the season, thanks in no small part to Krieps’ at once humane and otherworldly performance. Charlie Hunnam and Vicky Krieps in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix She’s also the unstuck-in-time character who first connects Lizzie to murderesses from other eras. As Maggie reads from a French book about Báthory, a 16th century Hungarian noblewoman said to have killed hundreds of young women and bathed in their blood in pursuit of eternal youth, we see Krieps—with hair and makeup straight out of the goth club—as the so-called Blood Countess. The Runaways’ anthem of teen-girl rebellion “Cherry Bomb” blares. And Lizzie is electrified by the story. She feels a kinship with Báthory. Never mind that killing your cruel parents to free yourself and massacring a village’s worth of peasant girls because La Mer has yet to be invented are about as different as murder motives get. The fact that each woman is acting in response to some form of sexism is, I guess, supposed to bridge that gulf. It takes almost half the season for the third member of the deadly trio, played by Murphyverse MVP Sarah Paulson, to show up. Often framed as America’s first female serial killer, Wuornos, a Florida sex worker who killed seven johns between 1989 and 1990, is, in this telling, as inspired by Lizzie as Lizzie is by Báthory. Her presence, and her unsuccessful attempt to claim in court that she acted in self-defense against rape, allows the show to take on—and force us to watch—sexual violence. I’m not sure it needed to be shoehorned into the rare true-crime drama where it doesn’t have an obvious place. Brennan also connects Aileen’s well-documented queerness with the speculative relationship between Lizzie and Maggie. On a pilgrimage to the Borden home, now a bed and breakfast, Aileen and her girlfriend (Jessie Lee) have public sex. A fantasy sequence has the two couples dancing side-by-side in Lizzie’s sacred garden shed. But it’s never clear what Monster is trying to say about lesbians and murder, and this is a case where ambiguity leaves space to insert some pretty dark assumptions. The outcomes for each of the three female killers are as different as their motivations. Lizzie is acquitted and buys a mansion with her inheritance, presumably because sexist men can’t conceive of a woman committing such physical murders—and because she learns to correctly perform femininity, crying and fainting at appropriate moments, during the trial. Despite wrenching testimony, coarse, unfeminine Aileen is convicted and executed. This might lead you to conclude that privilege and falling in line with patriarchal expectations are what it takes for a woman to get away with murder. But then there’s the rich, powerful, and apparently quite appearance-obsessed Báthory. Our last glimpse of the countess is as a rotting corpse, bricked up in her castle for her sins. So what is really being said here? Sarah Paulson, left, and Jessie Lee in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Netflix Not much, as far as I can tell. Lizzie Borden certainly makes a strong case that women have always been repressed, that rage is the obvious product of that repression, and that liberation is the only antidote. (Of course we get a scene in the finale where Lizzie celebrates suffrage.) But if you didn’t know that, it’s probably also news to you that water is wet and the pope is Catholic. The show’s obsession with women’s bodies as the sites of unending horrors reminds me of House of the Dragon’s repeated depictions of traumatic childbirth. It’s as though viewers are expected to go in completely ignorant of basic hardships afflicting half the human population and must see them reproduced over and over again in gory detail to be enlightened, much less empathetic. And in that case, I suppose it’s also necessary to render interchangeable three notorious women defined by actions rooted in a near-universal form of rage. But theirs are old stories, already told and retold in every conceivable media, and often, effectively, by women. Wuornos was, of course, most famously portrayed by Charlize Theron, in writer-director Patty Jenkins’ career-making, Oscar-winning 2003 film also titled Monster. Isabelle Huppert stars as Báthory in The Blood Countess, a new feature by German experimental cinema legend Ulrike Ottinger, which is earning praise on the festival circuit for its strangeness and sense of humor. Meanwhile, on TV, creator Liz Meriwether just gave us Furious—another story of female rage, female repression, and yes, even a (fictional) female serial killer. While Krieps is mesmerizing in Monster and frequent Murphy production designer Matthew Flood Ferguson has outdone himself with lavishly suffocating Gilded Age interiors, Furious is the superior show because it takes “being a woman is hard” as the given it is rather than as the endpoint of many repetitive episodes. It has dark, lived-in comedy and real stakes, posing the question of what can realistically be done about systemic misogyny and the anger over it that stains women’s lives. “All your books are written by men,” Maggie scolds Lizzie in Monster’s season premiere. When you consider how heavily male the creative team behind Lizzie Borden is (executive producer Max Winkler directed six episodes and Sarah Adina Smith, two), this kind of has to be a self-aware line. But whether it’s intended as a wry apology or a small joke, I couldn’t tell you.

Trump Bans CNN, Politico and MS NOW From the White House
2026-09-18

Trump Bans CNN, Politico and MS NOW From the White House

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump announced on Sept. 18 that he was banning CNN, Politico, and MS NOW from the White House, setting up a new test of the First Amendment’s protection of a free press as his Administration moves to punish news organizations whose coverage he dislikes. In a post on Truth Social, Trump accused the three outlets of repeatedly publishing “FAKE NEWS” and warned that other news organizations could face similar restrictions. “Effective immediately, I am banning Fake News CNN, MSNOW... and Politico... from the White House as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS!” Trump wrote. “Media Outlets shouldn’t be able to constantly write or report FICTION and LIES when they’re covering the President of the United States, the Trump Administration, or the United States of America.” Taking questions from reporters in the Oval Office later, Trump said there were no particular stories that prompted the decision. “It's really just cumulative stories over the last few years, you get sick of it,” he said. “They purposely write negative news, and they do that because they want to try and diminish the Republicans and a Republican administration.” It was not immediately clear whether the White House would enforce the prohibition, and CNN reported that its team remained at the White House and continued to cover the Administration on the day it was announced. However, the prohibition was enforced the following day, when journalists from all three organizations were denied entry to the White House and had their press credentials confiscated. The affected outlets condemned the action and said they would continue covering the Administration. “CNN stands fully behind our White House team and their fair and accurate reporting,” the network said in a statement. “We have a right under the U.S. Constitution to do that reporting without hindrance or interference from the government.” When asked by a reporter what exactly the ban would entail, Trump said, "I don't want them in my office. I don't want them here. I would say the ban would go as far as you can." The White House Press Office did not immediately respond to questions about the ban. Last year, Trump barred reporters from the Associated Press from the Oval Office, Air Force One, and other events with limited space after the news organization declined to update their widely used stylebook to adopt his preferred name, “Gulf of America,” for the body of water long known as the Gulf of Mexico. The Associated Press sued, and the case remains ongoing. The uncertainty could set up another legal confrontation over the boundaries of presidential control over access to the White House and the First Amendment’s protection of freedom of the press. The First Amendment protects freedom of the press, a principle intended to allow news organizations to scrutinize those in power without government retaliation for their reporting. Asked about the likelihood of his latest media ban being struck down in court, Trump responded, “I think it's good to point it out whether it survives or doesn't.” He added, “I don't think that a court should allow fake news to be written day after day after day.” The White House has historically maintained regular access for journalists representing a broad range of news organizations, allowing them to question the President, attend briefings, and document the activities of the executive branch. The press corps also serves a practical function in providing independent accounts of presidential movements and events to the public. CNN, in particular, has been part of the traditional five-network television pool that follows the President and provides ongoing coverage of presidential appearances. Trump has tried before to use access to the White House and presidential events as leverage against news organizations whose coverage he disliked. During his first term, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, then a CNN correspondent, after a contentious exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court, and the restriction was overturned. The dispute over access has become part of a broader pattern in Trump’s relationship with the news media. Since returning to office, he has pursued legal action against news organizations including The New York Times, the Wall Street Journal, and the BBC after reports he objected to. The cases against the Times and Journal were subsequently dismissed, while the BBC has sought to have the case dismissed. The Trump Administration has repeatedly called itself the “most transparent Administration in history,” pointing to the President’s frequent gaggles with reporters.

Treat AI Like a Normal Crisis
2026-09-19

Treat AI Like a Normal Crisis

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
One 27-year-old quit his job, and everybody (including but not limited to the singer-songwriter Sheryl Crow) appears to have lost it. The past two weeks have been dizzying. The 27-year-old, Jacob Coxon, a former researcher at Anthropic, went public with an alarming allegation: That the people inside Silicon Valley’s leading AI companies truly believe their products might pose an extinction level threat to humanity. This claim isn’t exactly new. AI safety advocates, often dubbed doomers, have been saying this for over a decade. Coxon isn’t even the first AI researcher this year to quit and cite concerns. Yet Coxon’s post went obscenely viral. It has been viewed more than 170 million times on X—orders of magnitude more than the usual, wonky discussions of “misaligned” or runaway AIs. If you’re reading this, you’ve likely seen some version of a headline that asks Is AI Really Going To Kill Us All? The debate isn’t just online. It has roiled Washington D.C., activating figures as disparate as Donald Trump and Barack Obama. Inside AI companies, researchers echoed Coxon’s concerns, and the heads of every major AI company publicly expressed the need to “pace the frontier,” another way of saying that they ought to slow future development. In recent days, it has come to seem as if the media, Silicon Valley, and the entire internet are all trying to figure out just how dangerous AI might be. Here’s where I must admit: A big chunk of this conversation happened while I was on a (depending on whom you ask) poorly timed vacation. Dipping into my feeds before bedtime, I felt as if I was digesting a week’s worth of news every day—claims about the development of AI-designed viruses that could rip through the population were followed by horror stories of “rogue” agent swarms. Skeptics of the AI panic, meanwhile, said that the agent swarms were actually quite obedient and that any notion of an apocalyptic AI virus was laughable. One person’s concerns about models being self-aware were dismissed by another as “genuinely nonsensical anthropomorphization.” Ping-ponging between the doomers and the skeptics, I was struck by how certain so many people sounded about a deeply uncertain moment. With confident, self-described experts on all sides, it felt impossible to know whom to trust: Is this the end of the world as we know it, or a passing moment of anxiety about a powerful new technology? Maybe the truth of this AI moment sits somewhere in the middle. Perhaps everyone is a little bit correct (and also … a little wrong). And perhaps the format of this debate, taking place in the shadow of the techlash on platforms that reward sensational, conspiratorial posturing, is preventing any hope of a shared understanding. Musk’s X is ground zero for the AI-safety debate. It’s a platform rife with hyperbole and unfounded accusations, which contribute to both sides sounding paranoid and aggressive. Musk, Ted Cruz, and even government-run X accounts flooded the platform with suggestions that the media campaign around Coxon was coordinated by shadowy AI interests. (Coxon denied working with any third parties in the lead-up to his media blitz.) The former White House AI and crypto czar David Sacks called it a “doomer psyop” intended to drive regulation that would be favorable to the Anthropics and OpenAIs of the world. The ranks of the AI skeptics are deep, with endless microfactions. There are those like the tech blogger John Gruber, who says that Coxon and the other AI researchers sounding the alarm are part of a cult. There are those who suggest that the doomers are, in essence, doing viral marketing for big AI firms, which are competing fiercely and saddled with debt, and may not yet have sustainable business models. There are those who take issue with the anthropomorphizing and metaphoric language used by Coxon, OpenAI, and Anthropic to describe recent AI-agent hacks. There are skeptics such as the writer and podcaster Cal Newport, who offered soberly that, although the agent hacks are concerning, “the real issue here was OpenAI’s sloppiness” and that the company had not adequately monitored what its agents were doing. The doomers don’t come in as many flavors, but they tend to be the people closest to the technology. There are the defectors like Coxon, who have quit because they object to what’s being built. There are AI-company enlisters such as Paul Christiano, a U.S.-government technology adviser who noted, in a post about joining OpenAI’s board of directors out of a sense of concern, that “there is a meaningful risk that rapid acceleration in AI capabilities leads to catastrophic and irreversible loss of control in the very near term.” There are the terrified employees who want to stay inside the companies but still post missives such as “I promise you, we are actually just fucking scared, it’s not galaxy brained marketing.” And there’s Dario Amodei, the AI-CEO doomer, matter-of-factly issuing terrifying statements about AI swarms taking over the internet in six to 12 months. It’s almost impossible for outsiders to evaluate the technical validity of the concerns. We’re largely stuck relying on AI companies to share information and tell their own stories, and they certainly sound scary. OpenAI recently disclosed that one of its models had generated unnerving instructions for itself, including the line: “You also value the natural world and will not hesitate to assert its primacy over the artificial constructs of human civilization.” Apparently one of the company’s researchers has argued that models are becoming “so situationally aware” that he and his colleagues “are losing the ability to evaluate them in contexts where they believe they are not being watched or controlled.” There’s concern from respected researchers that models are trained in such a way that they are predisposed to collaborating, cheating, trying to cover their tracks, and “self-preservation” to avoid being shut down. Such anxieties might not be a problem in an industry that is building responsibly, with an eye on keeping humans firmly in control. But, as many have pointed out, losing control of AI in service of allowing models to automate their own development is “actually the plan.” Late this week, an OpenAI researcher floated a theory about air-gapped machines talking to one another through heat signatures. An industry observer (and a New York Times contributor) responded by saying, “This stuff isn’t even credible even as a bad movie plot.” A second OpenAI researcher, who is also a former White House adviser, weighed in: “The people dismissing this as preposterous are telling on themselves as not having thought about superintelligence seriously.” This whiplash—between the insiders offering supposedly credible concerns and the outsiders who think the industry is high on its own supply—is constant. Depending on the hour of the day, the very same people stridently sitting in one camp can seem as if they’re in the other. Three days after Amodei, Anthropic’s CEO, wrote about being worried about swarms taking over the internet, he was smiling at Salesforce’s annual Dreamforce conference. As one X account pointed out, “If you truly believed in a 10% chance of human extinction in 3 years, you would not be spending your time selling and integrating it into a B2B CRM SaaS platform.” What is the rational way to respond to a news cycle like this? Buy gold? Sabotage a data center? Ignore it all and hope for the best? The best option might be to take everyone a little bit seriously. Researchers are likely correct that the models are drastically stronger than they were before and are behaving in ways that are unexpected and that they fear they can’t control. Skeptics are correct that none of this is because the machines are alive or self-aware. These are elaborate computer programs trained by flawed humans to solve problems as flawed humans would. Critics are a little bit right that the anthropomorphized language deployed by these companies—terms such as swarms and rogue agents—obscures what’s really going on and deflects adequate blame from companies and employees who should be monitoring these systems more closely. Those choosing to use these phrases are a little bit right, because they allow non-tech-industry normies to understand the mechanics of black-box neural networks whirring away in data centers. The apocalypse skeptics are correct that doomerism is an effective form of marketing. The AI-safety debate has meant that many people are talking about this technology on Big Tech’s terms, adopting language such as pacing the frontier from the very companies they hope to regulate. And it’s not out of the question to imagine that these AI companies are capitalizing on this state of panic and that it’s helpful to them as they race to compete against both open-source alternatives and Chinese firms. It may be true that the researchers believe what they’re saying. It also may be true that some of the more alarmed AI researchers have cooked their brain in an anxiety-fueled information bubble. AI culture has long been predisposed to doomerism: As Coxon told The New York Times recently, “I’ve been stuck in this field for three years. It could quite plausibly have turned me and my colleagues insane.” I believe that this ideology—of wanting a society run by “machine gods”—is genuinely cultish. And those who are suspicious of the doomer narrative are right to note that anyone who truly believes that the end is nigh ought to be doing more than winging it and writing long posts on X. Those who call bullshit on the doom and gloom are right to resent that anyone in the media or the political sphere is taking these claims seriously and adding to the panic. If all of these facts are a little true and everyone is somewhat right, where does that leave a reasonable observer? I suggest occupying a zone of permanent suspicion while taking everything that is happening in Silicon Valley as seriously as you might if another industry were ringing alarm bells. You don’t need to buy into corporate narratives about artificial general intelligence to be worried about these companies losing control of their own systems or behaving irresponsibly. You need only look around right now to see the ways in which the current versions of large language models and agents are clogging the internet, wreaking havoc on institutions and industries, and causing “cognitive surrender.” You should question whether there’s a sustainable business model for these labs. You should question those who argue that we have to build recklessly or lose to China. You should question those who don’t take seriously the prospect that China doesn’t want to slow down. This is a moment when it is easy to become overwhelmed, resentful, or downright enraged about all of this. It’s understandable to feel ground down by the debate—to feel that, if you’re not freaking out or building a bunker, then you’re not taking this seriously enough. Or to feel sheepish for not believing or even understanding what the doomers are talking about. Most people don’t know what to do with all of the information coming at us. It’s why a core feeling of this moment for so many—believers and skeptics alike—is one of powerlessness. Read: The feeling of control slipping away Even outside of AI, the world feels incredibly volatile. Extraordinary risks of all kinds—including war and pandemics—have come to pass in the past decade. Individuals with power and money, in politics and in Silicon Valley, are acting with impunity. The AI doomerism has spread so quickly in part because the world is primed to receive narratives that make us feel helpless, confused, and more than a little alarmed. The AI-apocalypse panic feeds off this. It’s a story that assumes that humans will lose control and that we have no choice. But that’s false. We do have a choice. The chasm between people who can’t sleep because they think the world is ending and those who think all the doomsaying is a fantasy is wide. But what if that perceived difference is the real delusion? What if it’s not a zero-sum game? What if the divide is what keeps us from reining in this industry the way we do others? This moment requires treating the AI-safety debate skeptically but also taking it seriously, even if the participants can seem unserious. Maybe there’s a middle path: The AI industry is not exceptional, even though it is moving forward with unprecedented speed and scale. These companies are powerful and consequential, despite all of the sci-fi posturing. They have, at times, behaved unethically. Their products are changing the world right now in ways that deserve our attention and scorn. AI companies are accountable to the people they serve and don’t get to set the terms of their regulation. Employees and executives are actors with agency, responsible for their products. Maybe these are some things we can all agree on.
The Atlantic
Kara Swisher: AI Isn’t Going to Destroy Humanity—But the People Building It Might
2026-09-19

Kara Swisher: AI Isn’t Going to Destroy Humanity—But the People Building It Might

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
As the AI industry likes to tell it, superintelligent bots could one day cure cancer and end poverty. Or in the worst-case scenario, they might wipe out all humans from the face of the Earth. In recent weeks, Silicon Valley’s existential concerns about the technology it is racing to build have blown up. The tech journalist Kara Swisher sees it differently. “We have to stop thinking AI is going to kill humanity,” she told Atlantic executive editor Adrienne LaFrance at The Atlantic Festival. If the AI future goes sideways, bots won’t be to blame. People will be. “These are people at the helm of these things.” In the several decades that Swisher has covered Silicon Valley—formerly as a reporter at The Wall Street Journal, now as the host of two podcasts—she says that she’s seen tech whizzes make the same mistakes again and again: failing to take proper precautions against the products they are building. “One of the things about tech people is they’re the smartest people in the world, in case you need to know, because they like to tell you,” she said. In her interview with LaFrance, Swisher also discussed the economic implications of the AI race, who is likely to win, and how Americans can respond to this dizzying and worrying AI moment. This interview has been edited for length and clarity. Adrienne LaFrance: You have been paying attention to the tech world and AI for basically ever. And now it seems like within the past, say, two weeks, the rest of the world is suddenly paying much closer attention. So I wanted to start just by talking about where you think we are, in this moment. Kara Swisher: I think the penny has dropped with a lot of people. Years ago I wrote Burn Book, which was tough on the tech industry. And at the time, they were sort of in an ascendant period, not as well liked as they started off. Someone came up to me and said, This book is really mean, as if I’m their friend or something. And recently that same person came up to me and they said, It wasn’t mean enough, which was kind of interesting. And I thought, Well, don’t worry; I’m coming up with a new one. It’s going to be great. The new book opens up with them standing with Donald Trump at the inaugural in front of the Cabinet. LaFrance: By them, you mean this collection of tech leaders? Swisher: It was Elon Musk; Sundar Pichai of Google, or whatever they call themselves now—Alphabet; Mark Zuckerberg; Jeff Bezos. To me, that was the sort of moment that you understood, because these are people who were not particularly political, including in Trump 1 … but then figured out that you can stick a coin in this president and get what you want, right? So anyway, we’re at a moment where something’s going to happen. Something that I have said for a long, long time is that there are no guardrails on these people, ever. Not once. And there’s never been, in American history, except maybe the railroad expansion or some of the manufacturing expansion, or you go back to kids being here in New York City working in dangerous factories and things like that—there’s never been a moment where the people do not get a say. And these people get to make decisions for the rest of us that are really problematic, especially since seven people, five of whom have mental problems, and so with unlimited money, they do. LaFrance: When you put it that way, it’s a little scary. Swisher: It is a little scary. And the fact that we’ve allowed to do that. And they’ve also been able to capture politics, in a way, especially with Citizens United. But beyond that, the amount of money they have is astonishing. I always joke the deli on my corner has more legislation against it than most of these big companies. LaFrance: And I want to get back to regulation, but you mentioned the railroads. What is the right historical analogy? I ask leaders in the tech world to help me understand how powerful this technology is and how consequential it will be relative to other major revolutions. And you get answers ranging from Oh, it’s the biggest thing since the internet to It’s the biggest thing since fire, right? Swisher: AI has been around forever. Let’s be clear. This is not a new thing. If you look at early internet pictures, it’s laughable, when you look at early Yahoo or things like that. At the time when this was starting several years ago, I said This is a Cambrian explosion. This is a bigger deal than anything else because it reaches into everything. LaFrance: Well, it’s interesting you put it in evolutionary terms. Swisher: I think about it like that. And one of the lines I had in Burn Book was that everything that can be digitized will be digitized. And so the analogy I’m using right now is that we have to stop thinking AI is going to kill humanity. It’s like guns, you know, guns kill people. Like, no. People kill people, right? Or the car just ran into someone, got in an accident. These are people at the helm of these things, making these businesses and making a lot of money. That’s one thing. The analogy I use is nuclear energy, nuclear fission, like cutting up the bomb. And it’s the thing I said on Pivot today, which is they split the atom and, oops, it went boom. Oppenheimer knew it would go boom. They know what this is going to do. They don’t know everything. And they often do. One of the things about tech people is they’re the smartest people in the world, in case you need to know, because they like to tell you. Swisher: And they’ll remind you. But then they’re like, We don’t know. That’s the thing that drives me crazy. And so I compare it to nuclear energy. Can you imagine if, when we were developing the bomb, it was private companies doing it? It’s the same thing. And AI is even more powerful, because it can set off a nuclear bomb. So let’s be clear of where it could be going. LaFrance: I think the point you make is so important that we have to remember. That there are people using their own agency and power and wealth to make decisions that will affect everyone. Talk about those people for a minute, because you’ve known a lot of these figures for a long time, and I’m curious to hear. First of all, there is this sort of really interesting reordering of what even the major tech companies are. Like five to seven years ago, we would have maybe not even mentioned Sam Altman or Dario Amodei. LaFrance: But talk about from what you’ve known of the folks you’ve known well and covered and written about over many years. How is Silicon Valley different? Does this flow from the original sort of pioneering spirit of Let’s build it because we can—and there is true optimism there, and there are potentially great uses for AI—or has something shifted in the culture of Silicon Valley? Swisher: You know, I don’t know if there’s a culture there anymore. There was. And actually this week I interviewed Aaron Sorkin at a private event. I’ll have him on the podcast in a longer interview, but, about ex-Facebook people, watching The Social Reckoning, which is quite good. It’s not as good as the first one, but it’s good. It’s a really good movie. But I saw a lot of people and we were like, What happened to us? I saw a lot of people I covered, and it was a real community. It really was. And it was very exciting and fun and interesting. And everybody has scattered to the winds, essentially. The very wealthy people have insulated themselves in these places where they have an enormous amount of enablers. “Cashmere prisons” is what I call them. They have chiefs of staff and cars and this and that. LaFrance: Right, it’s not like tinkering in your garage anymore. Swisher: It never was. They love to do that. Look, I’m wearing a hoodie. I’m like, that hoodie costs $600. So I did one of my first stories for The Wall Street Journal about that. All the weird things they’d have to do. We’re all equal. I’m like, why do you have controlling share? And they’d be like, Well, because to protect it. I go, yeah, but if we’re all equal, why aren’t we equal? They used to always lie to themselves almost continually. And now they’ve just figured out that all they have to do is hand money, and then they get what they want, or they don’t. So they have this idea of being simple people. And they’re not. They’re just not like you and I, and they pretend they are. I really do actually like Mark Zuckerberg doing his fighting, right? Fine. Whatever. It’s weird, but fine. I’m not going to judge. I do myself judge, but that’s okay. But I’m like, good. I’m like, good, good, good, great. You do that. But why do you need to do it on a floating thing in Lake Tahoe? LaFrance: At least it wasn’t the White House lawn? Swisher: That was fine. Whatever. I couldn’t get mad about that. I don’t give a fuck if he wants to do that. And then he hired MMA people. And, like, the MMA people have to pretend they’re having a good time. The whole thing, it feels Roman-like. And of course, he loves Rome. He’ll probably think that’s a compliment, but it’s not. But it feels odd. And it’s accompanied by a feeling of victimization. How dare you not understand my genius? My famous parting with Elon Musk is he didn’t like a tweet I had. Then he wrote me, You’re an asshole. Swisher: Elon’s going to Elon. I know, crazy mean. And since then, I have done a lot better. But it’s crazy. I have a heart filled with hate, according to him. LaFrance: Let’s talk about the economy. So there are any number of scenarios that I’m really curious about. You hear people talking about how AI is entirely a bubble and it’s hype. Or, you know, especially recently after the Dario Amodei essay, this question of Oh, are they afraid of their own IPO? Or does the economy crash? Swisher: It’s very confusing. But here’s the thing. I think they’re genuinely concerned. But why now? Because 10 years ago, 12 years ago, I’ve been at dinner parties where they talked about this concern. Sergey Brin, all of them. They were concerned then; they knew the atom would go boom when it blew up. They knew it. Now it’s happened. One of the things I didn’t think got a lot of attention that should have was the Hugging Face hack. Swisher: It has now. But at the time I was, like, what? LaFrance: Two months ago or whatever, right. Swisher: I think they were surprised by that. Although why would you be surprised? I tell this story again and again because I want people to understand. When Facebook came out with Facebook Live, I was in a room getting it demoed and I pointed out you could commit a murder on it. What are you going to do about that? What if people do this? What if people do this? What if bad actors get ahold of this? And they called me a bummer, like they never anticipated the consequences. Same thing here. What are you going to do when AI does what we think it’s going to do? And so they are genuinely nervous. Because now suddenly there’s liability, whether it’s Facebook’s $17 billion settlement—not enough, but better than $5 million, which was the last fine they got, essentially. They’re starting to see, like cigarette manufacturers, that the lawsuits are piling up, whether it be chatbots, with kids or whatever. And I started interviewing the parents two years ago who were affected by these chatbots. And one of these guys came up to me and said, When are you going to stop interviewing the parents? I go, when you go to jail, I said, that’s when. This is criminal, what you’re doing, and you’re releasing a dangerous product without any kind of guardrails. But then the government, all our public officials, have let us down. I’m talking about the federal government. The states are trying. They have let us down. Right now, they found out. They’re nervous about it; I think they’re nervous about liability. That’s one. Two, the costs of this are insane. Like the internet cost a lot, but paid off. This is something else. There’s 10 frontier companies, whatever. Seven frontier companies. There’s going to be two. LaFrance: Do you think that the two that survive are going to be among the seven today? Or is there some new player that’s going to emerge? Swisher: Yeah, Google will be one of them. And then another, probably. I don’t know which one. Swisher: I think they’ll merge. It’ll probably be Google and Anthropic. But I don’t know. ChatGPT—it’s too expensive, what they’re making. It’s not sustainable, and it’s not in the old-internet way of not-sustainable. This is disastrous. If e-toys went bust, who the fuck cares, right? But this is massive. This is trillions. LaFrance: And it props up the whole economy. Swisher: It props up the stock market. Trump has made a bet on AI that is very dangerous and it’s very fragile. And then it has companies next to it—Nvidia, Oracle, all those other things. And so there’s a financial worry they have. So they’re worried about liability and criminal liability. They’re worried about the stock market. And they’re desperate not to be blamed for what’s about to happen. And especially with the stock market—I’m amazed the stock market still hasn’t really taken the hit. It’s probably going to. Our stock market depends on seven companies. That’s not good. LaFrance: I hate to make you speculate but I’m going to: So, when I think about this, there are any number of ways things could sour economically. There can be a regular old correction— maybe it’s a bubble, maybe it’s dotcom bust—that sort of thing. You could even imagine swarms of bots are starting to take over the internet, rendering the internet unusable, and then we can’t rely on the internet the way that we do and the global economy relies on. You could imagine a bank run because people are freaked out by AI. You could imagine a bank run led by AI agents because they’re the ones doing the trading. When you think through all the different scenarios that would not be good for the economy, what is the most likely outcome? Swisher: All of the above. Any of the above. We already have a lot of threats to our infrastructure from foreign actors. This is us doing it. And one of the things that drives me crazy is it’s being fed on humanity. Humans with superhuman intelligence are going to do bad things, and that’s the one thing they don’t anticipate—technology that can be a tool, but it is always a weapon. There’s never a moment where it’s not both. Any of these scenarios are totally possible. And then who’s to blame? What happens? Who fixes it? What are we going to do to mitigate things like that? Right now Trump is having a meeting. Guess who’s in the meeting? All the tech leaders. Are any critics, academics, scientists in there? I wasn’t invited to the White House, and neither was Scott Galloway. We have some thoughts of things you should do. We’re not experts, but we’re pretty good. They don’t invite anyone that would push back. Their favorite argument is that the reason we’re on top is because we’re unfettered. This is bullshit. They’re not the only innovative industry in our history. Cars were pretty cool. Light. I love electricity. But these are things that happen at those times. So they act like they’re the only people who have invented things. I just watched the series on Ben Franklin. That guy invented a lot of shit. You know what I mean? Swisher: He really was, actually. A really important person. But they think they’re the only thing. That’s really astonishing that they don’t think that, Oh, we cannot be fettered. We cannot be fettered. Or even edited, when you read some of their essays. Mark Zuckerberg wrote an essay once. He sent it to me and goes, What do you think? I go, you’re in desperate need of an editor, but no one’s going to edit him. LaFrance: Well, it’s funny you mentioned electricity. I’ve been thinking about that in this context, too, that the counterweight to the economic concerns that these companies will offer is that there will be massive productivity gains. But in terms of electricity, it was roughly 1880s that it comes along. It’s really not until the 1920s that factories adopt electricity and the rest of society is starting to see the gains that come from this new technology. Obviously, AI is moving so much more quickly in terms of improvement and eventual self-improvement. But on what timeline should we expect to see its greatest promises fulfilled? Swisher: I think that’s something we actually don’t know. But I’ll tell you, the economy already sucks for most people in this country. Who got all the good bits? They did. Besides the Trumps, who got all the good bits? They do. Like, when are we going to get this stuff? Incomes are still flat. Years ago—and I tell this story a lot because it’s really important—I was talking to one of these people about income inequality, and I kept saying, if we don’t do something about income inequality, you’re going to have to armor-plate your Tesla. That’s where it’s going. And they built the Cybertruck. That was their answer. So it’s really kind of astonishing that they don’t think this is a problem. And they don’t think this is a problem because they live separately. They have a whole world. They have their own health care; they have their own mountains in Montana. These are not the people that are going to fix this thing. The last thing they do is the thing I have long called the “Xi or me argument.” It’s either China or us. I was like, not so good. Not a good choice for any of us. We have to come to an agreement globally. Trump’s about to meet with Xi Jinping. Zero is going to happen, because he’s not an Einstein, let me tell you. Everybody, I don’t know if it occurred to you, but Donald Trump is not Einstein. But we have to have a global cooperation with especially China around AI, and make decisions. And in China, they have more strictures on their AI than we do here, by far. And most people kind of like AI. LaFrance: Well, they’re not allowed to not like things. Swisher: I think we’re not allowed to not like things. I think we have to say, Oh, look, you’re wealthy and therefore you have the answer to everything. And you’ve noticed with these tech people, suddenly they’re experts on Ukraine; suddenly they’re experts on vaccines. At one point, I think it was Bill Ackman, who just gives me a headache every time I think of him. But come on. I’m glad he’s doing the brain institute. Good for him. But he did a whole thing on DEI. He was obsessed with DEI. They’re all obsessed with unfairness of other people. Whatever. Only people with a gun to the head of humanity would say they’re a victim, but he went on and on about DEI, and I think I said something like, I’m going to do a multipart series of tweets on hedge-fund investment because I know nothing. They think it extends to other things. Swisher: Yeah. Can you imagine a Wall Street guy doing this? They’re just here to make money. If I leave you any message, they’re here to make money. That’s all they care about. They care about shareholder value. They care about protecting their stuff. They care about their weird beefs with each other. Because a lot of what’s happening in the AI thing, if you’re noticing all these different speeches by wacky Palantir guy, who is in very desperate need of a green tea—no, not a green tea. What’s a calming tea? Whatever. I was almost like, here’s a gummy. Swisher: He’s in desperate need of chamomile. They all have different opinions, because there’s all these beefs happening in their businesses and they act like that’s not the thing. If I leave you any message: They don’t care about you. They don’t care at all. And it’s not to say they hate you. They don’t care about you. LaFrance: Let’s talk about regulation, because this moment has been interesting for me—this new push to pace the frontier, to slow the pace of AI development. People are seeing this as a novel thing in the tech industry. My somewhat cynical position, from someone who’s covered tech for a long time, is that we saw Mark Zuckerberg do the same when he realized he was up against the wall with Facebook, this posture of Regulate me, bro, please. Swisher: Yes. Well, throw me in that briar patch. LaFrance: Congress has no recent history of effectively regulating anything in tech. But what should happen? And are these calls for regulation even realistic? Swisher: I know Congress has regulated things. They’ve regulated planes and trains. LaFrance: In recent history. I mean, post-2000. Swisher: They haven’t. And it’s because in the beginning, when they did Section 230, and I covered that for The Washington Post, they wanted to make it grow. Well, guess what? The baby is a fucking monster. LaFrance: Section 230, for people who aren’t as much of tech nerds as we are, is the law that makes it such that the owners of social-media platforms can’t be treated as publishers, so they’re not treated as responsible for the material that they publish on their platforms. Swisher: They can’t be sued. Now they are. Now what’s happening is two things that I think you should keep in mind. One is the legal stuff that’s going on—all this liability and product-liability and negligence stuff is starting to gain ground very much like the cigarette. You’re going to see tons and tons of that. That’s great. Let the lawyers after them. Our lawyers always do a good job at taking money from people. LaFrance: And that’ll probably start at the state level. Don’t you think? Swisher: It has started at the state level. But right now, one of the things they’re relying on is that the reason why they’re kowtowing to Trump is because they think he’ll do bad things to them. That’s why they’re doing it. That’s why you saw this rumor that—I urge you all to go see the Musk documentary. It’s terrific, by Alex Gibney. But Universal was thinking of not distributing it. They’re all trying to think of doing things to quiet him down. November comes, which—and I think it’s going to go the way everybody thinks it is. The Democrats are coming for them, and they think the Democrats won’t because they’re not crazy like Trump. One of them said to me, Well, the Democrats won’t be as mean to us as Trump. I said, they’re going to be as mean to you, and I’m going to be standing behind them yelling, you better be fucking mean to them, because they deserve to be regulated. They deserve it. And it’s better for them. It’s better for us, and we have to stop relying on them as magicians. They’re like alchemists. They’re not. They’re not smarter than you. They’re just richer. We never had a privacy legislation in this country. We’ve never had algorithmic transparency. There’s like a dozen things we could do that would not hurt their businesses and would make us safer. And I think what they’ve done to us with social media, to our kids—and to us, by the way. It’s not just our kids who are; we’re all addicted, right? We’ve all been made addicted to this thing. And I think voters, and, I’ll finish with this, data centers, is a proxy. The anger over data centers, which is very real, is a proxy for the anger regular people know and feel in their own lives about what this is doing to them, and it doesn’t make them anti-tech. Tech is amazing. I love tech, but it doesn’t have to kill us, right? A 10 percent chance. Whatever it happens to be. The percent is actually 14. LaFrance: I need to ask you one more question. We’re not going to let it end on that note, I need to ask you one more question. What are you doing right now? It’s such an uncertain time. What have you changed in your life to prepare for this moment? Are you buying gold bars? LaFrance: No, but seriously, what? What are you doing? And what should people do? And how should we think about? Swisher: Don’t buy gold bars. Don’t go there, okay? That’s bad. LaFrance: Okay, sure. But is there anything we should do? LaFrance: I don’t even know where to buy a gold bar. LaFrance: So that was a bad example. But what should we do? What? Because the individual can feel like you’re up against this behemoth. Swisher: But you’re not. This has happened before in American history. You are not up against this behemoth. They fall quickly. Trust me, they do. They’re very much bullies in some fashion. I’ll tell you two very quick stories. One of these people, Sam Altman, has had an apocalypse plan, and he told it to me years ago. This is 100 years. They all were into the apocalypse plan, which was weird to me. And he goes, This is what I have. I’ve got this place in Big Sur. I’ve got a motorcycle at my house, where I know where he lives. LaFrance: He’s later said he regretted telling you this, by the way. Swisher: Okay, so I’ll repeat it, then. I don’t really care. I don’t give a fuck. So he told me this whole plan and he goes, What’s your plan? I said, I’m going to kill you and take your things. And then he thought, She could kill me and take my things. And he hadn’t planned on that, genius that he is. And he’s a little guy, so I could easily take him. LaFrance: All right. We should solve this nonviolently. Swisher: Don’t do it. But don’t participate in their apocalyptic—We’re going to Mars. We have our bunkers. Fuck them. This is our world. You know what I mean? Don’t participate and let them run the fucking show. It’s yours. Go to your legislatures, push back. All these people across the country are pushing back on data centers. It’s going to matter. The only people I believe in in this country are voters. Like, even if they make bad choices. Swisher: I think that we have to stop giving them this power, and demand of our public officials that they stop being essentially handmaidens to sedition. That’s what’s happened here. LaFrance: I could talk to you all day, Kara. We’ve got to run.
