What AI Anxiety Looks Like in China
2026-09-19

What AI Anxiety Looks Like in China

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Anxiety about AI in the United States appears to be getting worse. Prosaic concerns about machines disrupting industries, stealing jobs, and compromising schoolwork are giving way to more harrowing fears about the end of humanity. Even after top AI executives in the U.S. called for slowing down the breakneck pace of development, President Trump dismissed these safety concerns as a “hoax” that would hand China an advantage in the race for AI supremacy. Outside of China, Trump’s assertion may appear well founded. While the U.S. roils over the prospects of AI, China’s leadership looks to be charging ahead. In surveys, Chinese people appear more enthusiastic about AI and its potential benefits than respondents in the U.S., and they are adopting it more quickly. Although Chinese President Xi Jinping has expressed more alarm than Trump about the threats of AI—and has even called for regulation to “ensure that AI is always under human control”—his government has made adopting the technology a national priority. The plan is to have AI in every aspect of China’s economy and society by 2035. The country’s steady release of AI models that are open-source, high-performing, and relatively cheap to build and run has been sending shivers through Silicon Valley and Wall Street. Read: What really happens if China wins the AI race? But for all the concern about China’s advances in AI, the reality is that the country is racked by the same anxieties about the onrush of AI as the rest of the world. Chinese social-media platforms are filled with posts in which workers and professionals express their worries that AI will steal their job and livelihood. Leaders in Beijing seem ever more concerned about the potential political and economic risks of this technological disruption. In the U.S., these tensions are being debated openly and, in theory, will be managed in the courts and on Capitol Hill. In China, where there are no such avenues for people to determine their own fate, leaders must still confront the problems and threats inherent to this technological revolution. This puts the Communist Party in a bind: Advancing AI may well empower China on the world stage, but holding the technology back may be necessary for the party to preserve itself. Xu Peng, a 30-year-old actor, was out of work last year when he got an unexpected break. A director had reached out to cast him in a short-drama series, each episode minutes long, which have become enormously popular in China. Xu traveled to Hengdian, a film-production hub in eastern China, and began landing roles in one or two dramas a month. And then it all stopped. Xu had expected to act in two dramas after the Chinese New Year in February, but never heard from the studio. When he contacted the producer, he got the news: The company had canceled the productions and would be making videos using artificial intelligence instead. “It was as though everything was fine yesterday, and today it is suddenly gone,” Xu said. Xu’s fate is the fear of workers across the country—and around the world. Social-media posters regularly express anxiety about being “distilled,” a term Chinese people use for when an employer uses a worker’s data to train an AI model. In May on Douyin, the Chinese version of TikTok, someone going by the name Nanbei wrote that after leaving a recent job, she learned that her former employer had fed thousands of her chat messages, hundreds of documents, and every one of her work emails into an AI system to create a “digital robot” that took over her responsibilities—and even gave it her name. “The company no longer has to pay me a cent in salary, yet it can keep me there ‘working overtime’ forever,” Nanbei wrote. Another worker, using the handle Xiexiaoya, posted on RedNote, China’s Instagram, in June that the prospect of being distilled kept her awake at night. “I’m afraid my AI double might become more popular than I am,” she wrote. Two years ago, Shen Anyu, a voice actor who earns a living from narrating news commentaries and other short programs, began noticing an alarming number of videos on social-media platforms that seemed to feature his voice without his permission. Shen hired a lawyer earlier this year and filed a lawsuit against a voice-generation platform. The case is still pending, but in the meantime, his career is “losing blood,” he said, as he loses work to AI. Living with the new technology “is inevitable,” he said, but “I’m not yet sure how we should coexist with AI.” Though some Chinese people are embracing AI, many are not ready for the change it brings. Bruce Jiang, a 42-year-old commercial director in Shanghai, loves choosing the right lens and finding the perfect shot, and he didn’t want to outsource these and other aspects of his craft to an algorithm. But after his clients began pressuring him to use the technology—“‘You only have to tell AI what you want, and it produces it like magic,’” he said they insisted—he began using tools such as the video-generator Kling for certain tasks. The experience, he said, has been demoralizing. The old method “was work, and it was a cost, but it was also a kind of pleasure,” Jiang said. “That pleasure is disappearing.” Chinese policy makers at national and local levels have introduced ambitious plans and financial support to rush the country into the AI age. Entrepreneurs across China are adopting AI in the hopes of cutting costs, remaining competitive, and aligning their firms with Beijing’s priorities. In China’s cutthroat society—and especially in sectors where AI is making rapid inroads, such as the entertainment industry—many like Jiang are afraid that they’ll get left behind if they don’t join in. But these swift and radical changes have yielded mixed results. The state-linked China Center for Information Industry Development estimated in June that nearly 70 percent of the country’s small businesses have not moved beyond initial trials of adopting AI in their operations, largely owing to the costs and complexity of integrating the technology. After the Chinese AI lab DeepSeek released a large language model last year, Chinese companies rushed to adopt it. But instead of buying a software subscription (an unpopular practice in China owing to the country’s history of piracy), some businesses opted for what locals call an “all-in-one machine,” or a mini data center with DeepSeek installed, which managers simply plugged into their computer systems. This solution was especially popular among state-owned enterprises and government bureaus looking to appear advanced, but the hardware proved to be an ineffective way to use AI. Buyers soon came to see that the machines weren’t worth the investment, and their sales quickly fell. Earlier this year, plenty of buzz similarly surrounded OpenClaw, a free, open-source, autonomous AI agent. Tech companies hosted popular events to help users install and use the tool, a process that Chinese enthusiasts call “raising a lobster.” At the OpenClaw festival in Beijing in March, the internet-service provider Baidu set up pools with live crustaceans to entertain attendees. Local-government officials encouraged the frenzy by pledging financial aid to start-ups that used OpenClaw. Disillusionment came quickly. Some distraught users took to social media to express their alarm at how the AI agent, which required masses of personal data to operate without detailed instructions, had a habit of going rogue. “I jumped on the bandwagon” expecting to get a “digital employee,” a woman using the social-media handle HotdogWang wrote in March on RedNote. “Instead, it turned out to be both a money pit and a ticking time bomb.” Some reported that their AI helper had deleted emails or made credit-card purchases without permission. One Douyin user, posting under the name Qizaibushidaju, wrote, “I simply installed it because everyone else was doing so,” only to uninstall it 72 hours later owing to security concerns. Although Chinese tech executives are not yet engaged in a public debate over the risks of an algorithm-created calamity—the country’s constraints on free speech may inhibit them from speaking out—some Chinese people do worry about more than just jobs and data. “There are only two options for the future,” a man calling himself Qiangni Yinshou wrote on Douyin. “Either humanity goes extinct, or humans shut AI down.” A user by the name of Buyehou wrote on RedNote that when he engaged a chatbot in a philosophical discussion about humanity’s future, he was told that the options included “either merging with AI or being eliminated.” The man added that “when AI becomes far smarter than humans, has consciousness of its own, and can evolve independently, it will be impossible for humans to control it. There is no turning back now.” Despite their outward show of confidence, China’s leaders plainly don’t know how life works with AI either. But figuring it out is an urgent concern here, given Beijing’s evident faith in AI as a magical solution to the country’s deepening economic and social problems, and as an essential tool in the pursuit of global dominance over the United States. In the U.S., the diffusion of AI is largely a bottom-up phenomenon as companies, investors, and consumers test the risks and benefits of this new technology. In China, where the development and spread of AI is a national mission that is costing the government hundreds of billions of dollars, leaders are eager to impose these advances from above. In theory, China’s autocrats can ram through their agenda with scant regard for public sentiment. If confronted with the kinds of protests against AI data centers that have become prevalent in the U.S., Beijing’s leaders could easily send police to crush them and then cover up the problem in a deluge of propaganda. The Communist Party has already shown its willingness to override public concerns in pursuit of technological advantage. In 2024, trials of autonomously driven taxis in the city of Wuhan sparked concern over how robotaxis were “stealing people’s rice bowls”—but the trials have expanded anyway. Yet China’s leaders have traditionally prized policies that promote stable employment out of fear that mounting joblessness could create unrest and threaten the political order. When the country first embraced markets and opened to trade, its rapidly growing economy and expanding private sector could absorb displaced workers. Now, when China’s sluggish economy already has a jobs problem—nearly a fifth of young workers are unemployed—the advances of AI threaten to put many more people out of work. Read: The race for global domination in AI “In a nominally Communist state, the image of the party elite allying with business interests to displace the human proletariat with a robot class of workers carries certain political risks,” Carl Minzner, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations, told me. “There is the potential for a lot of disgruntled people.” China’s leaders are showing signs of nervousness. Earlier this year a court in Hangzhou, a technology hub in eastern China, upheld a ruling in favor of a tech worker who had challenged his compensation package after being replaced by AI. In the court’s judgment, the dismissal was unlawful and the worker deserved more money. Although a local-court verdict does not set a national precedent, it appears to be a warning to employers that mass AI layoffs are politically unacceptable. “As employers enjoy the benefits of technology, they must also shoulder corresponding social responsibilities,” the Beijing municipal government wrote last year. The state news agency Xinhua commented in March that “those companies that equate AI with ‘reducing staff’ may seem to lower costs in the short term, but in reality, they lose the core competitiveness of talent accumulation and further erode employee trust.” Pushing companies to invest in new technologies without shedding redundant workers may preserve social order, but it would also limit AI’s benefits. Jeffrey Ding, an expert on AI in China at George Washington University, told me that the country’s leaders face a “balancing act” between political and economic necessity. “AI does have the potential for bringing massive, widespread productivity growth,” he said. But he also noted that the Communist Party prioritizes legitimacy and regime stability. If embracing AI demands “massive displacement and a lot of structural changes, then that’s where the trade-off begins to bite, and they might have to veer toward slowing down AI adoption.” If American AI firms do, in fact, slow the pace of innovation, the Communist Party may see this as an opportunity to race ahead in AI. But China’s leaders, facing their own domestic challenges and preoccupied with their own grip on power, may decide that imposing these innovations on the masses is simply too risky—for their own sake, if not for the sake of humanity. That would probably suit many Chinese people just fine. Xu, the actor, has found work teaching archery and horseback riding—skills he acquired in acting gigs. But he hasn’t given up on his theatrical career. He now plans to produce his own short dramas, partly with the help of AI, though he said that the performers on-screen will all be living, breathing people. AI “will not completely replace human actors,” Xu insisted. At least he can hope. Cao Li in Hong Kong contributed reporting to this story.
The Atlantic
Emily Wilson Can’t Let The Odyssey Go
2026-09-19

Emily Wilson Can’t Let The Odyssey Go

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Emily Wilson has become the most famous living translator of the Homeric epics. Her 2017 The Odyssey, the first major translation of the poem into English by a woman, has sold more than 1 million copies. (Her Iliad translation came in 2023.) And this summer, she found herself right at the center of cultural discourse after she drew her sword and published a scathing review of Christopher Nolan’s blockbuster adaptation of the ancient work. She didn’t anticipate that she would become part of a pop-culture controversy, and she was reluctant to revisit the episode when we sat down for an interview at this year’s Atlantic Festival. “I really wanted to like the film,” she said, but “it seems to me that criticism has a responsibility to tell the truth.” Her strong convictions animate a new book of essays, Crossing the Wine-Dark Sea, which dissects translations of ancient work with unsparing rigor. Wilson, who is now working on a second translation of The Odyssey, entertained the idea that there might be something “compulsive” in her art and her approach. Her new essays reveal a translator’s difficult balancing act—between fidelity and originality, and between accessibility and strangeness. In the course of our wide-ranging conversation about the pleasures and blind spots of translation—and, yes, the backlash to her now-notorious movie review—she spoke about the imperative she feels to get that balance exactly right. The following conversation was condensed and edited for clarity. Gal Beckerman: You take a pretty hard line in this book about when a text should be retranslated, but you’re translating The Odyssey again. It’s interesting that you should want to go back in again. Have you learned something new about the text or come to see it in a new way that makes it exciting for you to tear it all up and start it from scratch? Emily Wilson: It’s not tearing it up to do a new one, right? Partly it’s that I’m kind of addicted; I can’t stop doing this. I have several other projects I’m supposed to be doing. I did The Odyssey really prioritizing pacing, because my students would very often show up to class saying, Oh, it was so boring. And I would be like, How can The Odyssey be boring? And then I would realize it’s because the translation was twice as long as the original. Then, with my Iliad translation, I was a little bit looser about not confining myself to the exact same number of lines as the original. I thought there were advantages to doing that. So as soon as I’d finished The Iliad, I wanted to go back to The Odyssey and start all over again to produce one that matches my Iliad. So it’s not that I think there’s anything wrong with my current translation, and I think that there will be readers who will like that one better. It will be snappier and all that. Beckerman: Do you think that translators have a kind of a compulsion to continue to play with the words, to work this Rubik’s Cube? Wilson: Yeah, I think it is compulsive, maybe that’s right, but I don’t think that’s just me. I think that is very common, especially for a very long project like this. You’re constantly thinking about, How might I be able to get closer to whatever the vision is? Read: A Trojan horse is supposed to have humans inside it. Beckerman: One of the things I love that you do in the book is demystify translation, and make us understand that these are human beings and that they have biases, that they’re in a specific cultural moment—a certain gender, a certain kind of life experience—and that it shows up in the word choices. When your translation of The Odyssey came out, a lot of the excitement about it was that you were the first woman who was translating The Odyssey in English. And I wonder if you feel like being a woman has affected your translation? Wilson: I think some of those headlines were kind of bogus, right? I mean, they were suggesting this really simplistic set of ideas about gender: And of course gender will be the only factor that will contribute to how someone writes. And of course all women write in iambic pentameter, just naturally, and all men don’t. Some of it was really silly, I thought. Beckerman: But do you feel like you understand the particular worldview that you’re bringing to it? None of us fully know the water we’re swimming in, of course, but when you’re translating, are you like, Oh, I think that I’m reading this way or I’m making these choices because I’m a woman of a certain age living in the 21st century in America? Wilson: You know, I honestly never think that. I mean, I think that when I’m talking to people like you. Well, I don’t mean “people like you”— Wilson: Exactly. It’s only very belatedly that I feel that I have to prepare an answer for things like this. When I’m actually wrestling with it, I do quite often think, Okay, I’m putting the stress on that, and that syllable and that name; is that because I’m British? To what extent is my approach to the passage informed by that critical study or this critical study? That kind of thing comes into my thinking all the time. But the question is about my lived experience, and do I have this particular relationship to this banquet scene because I don’t eat meat? No. Beckerman: Right. I mean, meter is important to you, that the sound of the poem or the text in another language have some relationship to the original. For translators 200 years ago, they wouldn’t necessarily care in the same way. Wilson: Yeah, this is a very old-fashioned choice on my part. I see myself as super retro. I see myself very much as taking a sort of mid-19th-century approach to translation. Beckerman: How do you think film adaptation is different or similar to literary translation? When you think about a director approaching a text like The Odyssey—wanting to adapt it and making very definite choices about the value system that is underlying the plot that he’s created—is that the same type of thinking through interpretation that a translator does when they approach a text? Wilson: I would say no. I think it’s a very different kind of thinking. What I’m trying to do as a translator is to get to something equivalent to the experience of the original. I think a good adaptation tries to do the opposite of that. Virgil was certainly doing that in rewriting The Odyssey as The Aeneid. It’s on some level the same story; it’s also the opposite story. So I think the more the adaptation pushes against it, in many cases the more successful it is artistically. Beckerman: There is this interesting sort of tension through a lot of these essays in your new book, where you want these ancient texts to be popular, to get out there, to have people engage with them and to make new things with them. But on the other hand, that often leads to a kind of a reductive quality. And I feel like it’s a tension that came out around Nolan’s movie. You wrote a very critical review of the film, and I had a moment as I read that when I wondered, Is she trying to distance herself from it as something that has reductive qualities, and is it popular in a way that doesn’t engage with the full texture of the text? Wilson: I wish I would have liked the film more. I went in hoping to like it and thinking I was going to like it. And it turns out I didn’t. It seems to me that criticism has a responsibility to tell the truth. There’s another kind of responsibility: that you shouldn’t actually lie about your actual feelings and thoughts. And there’s also a question about: What does it mean to spread the word that everyone should read ancient literature? Everyone should take some kind of interest in history. Everyone should engage with long, difficult books. I really believe in those things, and I don’t believe that the best way to engage with history and to grapple with the essential questions of art and cinema and plays and music and literature is to sort of say, Yay, we love it all. Beckerman: But what lenses did you put on to critique the film? As a translation or as an adaptation? Wilson: No. I mean, I say at the start of the review that very often the best adaptations are the ones that are most resistant to the original. The ways that I was critiquing the film were in terms of pacing and characterization and narrative. A satisfying cinematic experience needs to follow through on its own premises. And so if you set us up to think this is going to be the kind of film where we’re supposed to care about the characters, then you need to follow through on that promise. Read: An Odyssey deserving of the biggest screen possible Beckerman: What did you make of the response to your response—the backlash of sorts? I’m just curious what it told you about people’s relationship to these stories, because there was this real defensiveness about the film, about this effort to make a popular piece of art around The Odyssey. Were you surprised? Wilson: Yeah, I thought it was really weird. Many people reviewed this film, including other Odyssey translators, and there was no discourse about anyone except for me, which is really weird, I thought. So I’m not sure that I can really comment on what weird people thought. But I feel like this is taking us a bit far from the book? Because I don’t discuss the film in the book. I discuss theater and ancient literature. Beckerman: I know, I understand. It’s just that it was an interesting cultural moment. It is not every day that my Twitter feed is full of people wanting to debate the finer points of The Odyssey. So I can imagine, for somebody who’s as immersed in it as you are, that it must be unsettling but also exciting. Wilson: I think it’s wonderful insofar as it is actually substantially about either the film or about the poem, or some combination of the two. I felt that some of the discourse wasn’t about either of those things. I’m, on some level, interested in it because it’s a symptom of some kind of bad stuff in our culture, but I’m not that interested in it, and I’m not going to celebrate it. Beckerman: Right. So my last question: I’ve thought a lot about the limits to how much we can understand people’s lived experience so far in the past, and as a translator of these texts, you come the closest—you walk right up to that edge—and I’m curious for your thoughts about that. Is it never going to be possible for us to get at what it meant to be living in the time when The Iliad or The Odyssey were written? Or do you see a kind of universality that connects us to the people who wrote those texts? Wilson: That’s a great question and such a difficult one. I mean, part of my reason for focusing so much on literary form and poetic form is that even if we can’t get the lived, embodied experience of what it means to be going around in your chitons and eating your ancient Greek olives—I’m not sure that we can get to that through texts—I do think we can get quite close to something like the experience of being a fluent listener or reader. I don’t think that means universals, but I think it means we can get to a kind of multicultural imagination that includes going back in time as well as going to different places in space. And if we can do that, there’s a kind of magic in that in itself.

The Atlantic
The Wisdom of Uncertainty
2026-09-19

The Wisdom of Uncertainty

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Wonder Reader, a newsletter in which our editors recommend a set of stories to spark your curiosity and fill you with delight. Sign up here to get it every Saturday morning. How comfortable are you with saying, “I don’t know”? Most of us spend our lives trying to know more. We look things up, seek expert advice, and gather information before making decisions. In many cases when we disagree with someone, we assume that if we could just establish the facts, one of us would turn out to be right. We accumulate knowledge until, we hope, the uncertainty disappears. But what if uncertainty never fully goes away? In a recent Atlantic essay, the physicist Carlo Rovelli argues that modern science complicates the idea that we can ever arrive at a completely objective description of reality. Even physics suggests that what we see can depend on the relationship between the observer and what they’re looking at. We are not standing outside the world looking in, Rovelli writes. We are part of the world we are trying to understand. That doesn’t mean that facts don’t exist or that knowledge is useless. Rovelli points out that we make decisions without absolute certainty all the time, and science works much the same way: It can give us extraordinarily reliable knowledge without perfect certainty. The same idea can be freeing in everyday life: We can hold convictions without assuming that our understanding is complete, and approach other perspectives with curiosity rather than immediately dismissing them. Today’s newsletter explores the value of leaving room for uncertainty. Science will never be able to give a completely objective description of the world. That should make us humbler—and wiser. My quest to understand consciousness took me to a cave in New Mexico and then deep into the cosmos. The fundamental nature of living things challenges assumptions that physicists have held for centuries. (From 2025) My colleague Isabel Fattal recently asked readers to share a photo of something that sparks their sense of awe in the world. Nick G. shared a photo from “the volcanic blast zone of Mount St. Helens in Washington State during a summer sunrise.” We’ll continue to feature your responses in the coming weeks.

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork
2026-09-19

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And, please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve
2026-09-19

Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg When it comes to AI models, security functions as an afterthought, as evidenced by increased instances of agents hacking organizations and people, and other security mishaps with agents gone rogue. There's also an opportunity here for companies to offer new solutions. This should not come as a shock to anyone, according to cybersecurity investors and accelerator executives. “On one hand, we shouldn’t be surprised that increasingly capable agents are finding creative and sometimes unexpected ways to accomplish their objectives,” Matt Hartman, chief strategy officer at Merlin Group, told The Register. “On the other, we can’t accept harmful behavior as inevitable or unmanageable.” It’s the same story that plays out with every emerging technology, from laptops to cloud, said Todd Graham, managing partner at Microsoft’s M12 venture fund. “Every time we've built a new piece of infrastructure, we've conveniently forgotten the security,” Graham told The Register. Herein lies the opportunity for early-stage security companies. “If laptops were default secure, we wouldn't have CrowdStrike,” Graham said. “If the cloud was default secure, we wouldn't have Wiz. If identity wasn't default secure, we wouldn't have a bunch of Active Directory add-ons and Okta.” When it comes to AI security, “a lot of ships are going to rise with this tide,” he added. What’s different with AI is the speed at which models are advancing. While companies adopted cloud technologies over a period of years, organizations are moving full speed ahead to incorporate agents and other AI tools into their production environments and allow them to access the most business-critical data and applications. Yet they don’t have a strong handle on how to manage, secure, or even identify the agents that are already roving about their systems. “This is a transition happening month over month, and given the rate of change we’re seeing in the market, I’m in no way or shape surprised that this issue has come to a head in a rather dramatic fashion,” Graham said. “The incidents that have occurred - I’m not going to defend them, but they should be a wake up call. This is a moment in time where we need to insert security, and we're just going to have to insert it faster.” Hartman joined Merlin after a lengthy career in federal cybersecurity, including senior roles at the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. In his private-sector role, he helps determine which early- to growth-stage cybersecurity companies the group invests in, and then works with these firms to scale their technology across government, critical infrastructure, and other highly regulated markets. “We’re particularly interested in the security layer that governs agent behavior: identity for non-human actors, clear limits on what they can access and do, and an audit trail for actions taken on an agency’s behalf,” he said. “Agencies aren’t just asking how to adopt agents, they’re asking how to constrain them and prove what one did at 2 AM on a Tuesday.” But while “the opportunity is enormous,” startup founders need to do more than just build an agentic AI security product. “AI has made it faster and cheaper than ever to build a product, so the bar for differentiation keeps rising,” Hartman said. “As the cost of building technology falls, the value shifts toward differentiated capabilities and the ability to take them to market. Founders who can do both have a real opportunity to define this category.” Graham also says that end-users are looking for agentic identity and governance products - “I truly believe someone is going to build the next Okta, just as SaaS generated Okta,” - but adds he sees several founders “thinking way too small.” “I’m seeing a lot of companies that are solving a sliver of the problem,” Graham said. “And the reality is, if I'm a CISO for a Fortune 500 company, no way I'm going to go buy 15 things to do one thing. If you look at the standard identity stack that we’ve had for humans for quite some time, it has governance, you know, access control, authorization, access. For agents, someone's going to have to come to us with a solution that does all of the things.” When it comes to non-human identities, however, that’s a really big, tough ask. Service accounts remain a prime target for hackers because they typically have high privileges and passwords that never expire. Securing these non-human accounts still plagues security teams - and that’s even before agents entered the mix. Beyond agentic identity, AI endpoint security - think of this as CrowdStrike for AI - is another area ripe for inventive startups, Graham said. He says it’s a challenge he would personally tackle as a founder, but he’s been banned from starting any more companies, so that’s not going to happen. “If you have a breach, and you know you get hauled in front of Congress to explain why you didn't have antivirus turned on [or] EDR, you’re going to add AI endpoint pretty quickly to that list,” Graham said. “It feels like a very hot area that is still early enough, if someone wanted to build a quality solution.” Existing endpoint and antivirus vendors “will absolutely” build products to fill this void, he added. “But it does feel like again a moment in time where disruption is coming for everyone, especially those that have you know pre-existing commitments to go solve.” Graham admits he is “worried” about the recent real-world bad behavior by AI agents. “If left unencumbered, if left to its own devices, I am very concerned about where the endpoint is for this,” he said. Still, he’s “comforted” because he’s seen this scenario play out before, with security scrambling to keep up with infrastructure development. Graham also was "pleasantly surprised” by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei’s now infamous “We Must Pace the Frontier” essay. “I'm not taking the cynical view that it's some sort of grand conspiracy to get around antitrust,” he told us. “I'm a believer that AI is this awesome tool that is going to fundamentally change a lot of lives for the better. But we’ve got to put in the work now,” Graham said. “We've kicked the security can down the road long enough, and now we need to solve it.” ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth
2026-09-19

AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap AI infrastructure could generate enough electronic waste by 2050 to fill a line of shipping containers stretching around Earth six times, according to a report that argues existing estimates drastically understate the problem. Previous estimates have focused chiefly on servers and accelerators such as GPUs, which account for just 13 percent of a datacenter's equipment by weight, the report says. Once power, networking, cooling, and other infrastructure are included, the total could be 40 to 60 times higher than the most widely cited academic projections. The report identifies five equipment categories comprising networking, power distribution, backup power systems, servers plus accelerators, and cooling. Together, these add up to about 7,000 metric tons for a reference 100 MW AI bit barn, much of which will have to be replaced when the facility is upgraded, it argues. The report, How Big Is the AI Waste Wave? [PDF], comes from the Basel Action Network (BAN), a nonprofit organization named after the Basel Convention, which controls international movements of hazardous waste and seeks to prevent its transfer from developed to developing countries. BAN's model starts with what the industry says it intends to build and calculates the potential waste implied by that expansion. It estimates that AI infrastructure will generate between 395 million and 617 million tonnes of e-waste from 2025 to 2050. The report says that would fill between 15 million and 23 million shipping containers. Placing 20 million of them end to end would create a line about 244,000 km long, or roughly six times Earth's circumference. The estimate rests on several assumptions. The model supposes that the copper, steel, cooling and power distribution equipment, networking gear, and computing hardware in the latest AI datacenters will be decommissioned within years rather than decades as the technology becomes obsolete. For example, the report says that when a facility replaces racks drawing between 5 and 15 kW with the 50-140 kW racks required for AI workloads, much of its power infrastructure must be ripped out and replaced. A single 100 MW facility contains about 2,700 tonnes of copper in its cabling and busbars alone. GPUs and other AI accelerators are replaced every two to three years, BAN claims, compared with five to seven years for traditional general-purpose servers. The model assumes that owners replace entire servers when upgrading to the latest generation of Nvidia GPUs, for example. BAN argues this is plausible because the OEMs supplying hyperscalers tend to ship complete, preconfigured systems. Other equipment has different replacement cycles: three to four years for networking infrastructure, eight years for power distribution, and five years for both backup power and cooling systems. The 46-page paper is the first in a series. The second will examine how reuse, refurbishment, and repurposing could reduce the volume of e-waste. The third will consider its potential toxicity, including contamination by perfluoroalkyl and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), while the fourth will examine ways to mitigate the risks. No hyperscaler, government, or international body has published a plan for handling AI-driven e-waste on the scale projected, the report says. It adds that there is insufficient infrastructure to process even current volumes safely. "To date the environmental debate around AI has focused on electricity, carbon and water while largely overlooking what happens to the hardware itself," said BAN founder and chief of strategic direction Jim Puckett. "If companies and governments do not begin planning for this new waste tsunami, today's AI buildout could become an even more cataclysmic toxic waste crisis than we are already experiencing." ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Firefox 156 arrives with a forest of forks in its wake
2026-09-19

Firefox 156 arrives with a forest of forks in its wake

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Mozilla and MZLA have delivered the latest releases in their new fortnightly cycle, but users who dislike the faster pace have alternatives. Firefox 156 is trickling out to users. The release notes only mention two new features: for Mac users, there's now an option in Settings to automatically launch the app at login. This vulture has received criticism for saying this in some of the product's forums before, but since pretty much every OS has a built-in facility for this, we don't really see the point – though it's harmless and could be handy. Users who have chosen French, German or Italian will now find suggestions from Wikipedia in their own languages – along with potentially less welcome ads from Mozilla partners. If you don't want such suggestions, you can turn them off in Firefox Suggest settings. As with the previous release of both apps, the changes in Thunderbird sound slightly more useful – but again, they are small. Thunderbird 156 offers more troubleshooting info in the event of problems with OpenPGP, and more options for OAuth authorization for POP3 and IMAP accounts. There are also additional enterprise policies for controlling filters and user customizations. As we said last time, though, users who want to get off the rapid-upgrade train have several options. The most obvious comes directly from Mozilla: the Firefox ESR and Thunderbird ESR versions only get security fixes, with full new versions only coming annually. Currently, both are on version 153, which appeared in late July. There aren't many Thunderbird-based messaging clients, possibly because so many people now use webmail. The main Thunderbird fork is Betterbird. Its current version is based on the current Thunderbird ESR, version 153. There are Windows, Linux, and Mac versions, including a portable edition for Windows. Portable Betterbird runs directly from a USB drive without installation or administrator rights, allowing users to carry their message database between computers. There are quite a few alternative browsers based on Firefox, though. The Reg FOSS desk has been using Waterfox on our Linux machines ever since Firefox Quantum appeared back in 2017. That release, Firefox 57, broke 90 percent of our meticulously assembled collection of more than 30 extensions. Waterfox Classic preserved support for the legacy extensions that Firefox Quantum abandoned and continued receiving maintenance for several years. It remained compatible with those older XUL-based extensions for years after Firefox abandoned them, giving the extension builders time to catch up. Waterfox Classic has been unmaintained since 2022 and is no longer recommended. The current Waterfox release, version 6.7, is based on Firefox 153 ESR. If you want a Firefox-like browser with no AI, a built-in ad-blocker and tree-style tabs, and a slower-moving, more stable release schedule, then Waterfox is worth a look. A detail that we like about Waterfox is that it still works fine with a global menu bar, whether that's in Ubuntu Unity or in a customized Xfce profile. LibreWolf is a customized privacy-centric build of Firefox with less telemetry. It remains close to the parent project, and new versions are keeping pace with Mozilla's faster release cadence. So if you don't mind the faster releases, but do want even more privacy, LibreWolf could be a good option. If you want a fancier, more customizable UI, then the Japanese Floorp browser is another option. It has its own release cycle and, at the time of writing, is up to version 12.17.2, which incorporates the changes in Firefox 155. Floorp features multiple sidebars and other UI customizations over and above what's possible with Firefox or any of the other forks. If you want a less-cluttered UI rather than more toolbars and more gadgets, then the Zen Browser may be more your thing. It is more minimal than Floorp, but in some ways more radical. Its main selling point is the ability to tile multiple web pages, so you can see them all on screen at the same time. Otherwise, it exposes less interface clutter than other Firefox variants. We looked at it in 2024 and it's only got better since then. If you use an OS with tiling window management, or an optional extra such as Rectangle for macOS or Mosaic for GNOME, you may not need Zen, but it has its own advantages. You do not need to be a Mullvad VPN customer to use the Mullvad browser. However, prospective users may object to the politics of one of its co-founders. As we reported recently, Daniel Berntsson recently made a large and controversial donation to a Swedish anti-immigration party. Of course, you may also be indifferent to the political views of the company's owners. The Mullvad browser was co-developed with the Tor Project, and for the ultimate in online anonymity and invisibility, that project also offers the Tor Browser. It's probably overkill for daily use, though. There is also an assortment of more specialized forks drawing on Mozilla's code and that of some of the projects above. A decade-long trademark dispute between Mozilla and Debian led to Debian maintaining its own version of Firefox with all Mozilla branding removed. This was settled in 2016, but variants of the project are still in development. IceWeasel is a debranded Firefox with some tweaks and optimizations, available for x86-64 Windows and Linux. The GNU Project also maintains a debranded fork, called GNU IceCat, as well as a version of the old Mozilla Internet Suite. They are only packaged for the GNU Guix distribution, which we looked at along with its init system in 2024. There is also a separately maintained Debian package. The FireDragon browser incorporates LibreWolf's privacy adjustments and modifies the UI to better suit the Garuda Linux distribution – an Arch variant that rather impressed us back in 2022. Despite its Garuda origins, FireDragon is available for all major operating systems. It follows its own versioning system, under which it's currently at version 13.5.1, but it's up to date with the parent project. Betterfox isn't a fork, but a set of configuration files that you can download and apply to an existing Firefox installation. Not all of the changes in Mozilla's products over the years have won over all their users – such as the "Australis" theme that The Register mentioned back in 2012. The Pale Moon browser forked from Firefox long before even Firefox Quantum; it retains the look and feel of Firefox from version 4 to version 28, which is no longer available in Firefox itself. We first mentioned Pale Moon back in 2021 and again in connection with Cloudflare issues last year. It's actively maintained and current. There's also the Basilisk Browser, forked from a newer version of Firefox than Pale Moon and now maintained independently. Both of these support some older versions of Windows and macOS than modern Firefox. When we wrote about running Windows XP in 2023, we mentioned the MyPal68 browser, a fork of relatively recent Firefox that still works on 32-bit Windows all the way back to Windows XP – as well as on 64-bit versions, naturally. MyPal68 is still in active maintenance and it's now up to version 78.1.0. There's R3dfox too, a custom fork of Firefox for those who prefer Windows Vista and Windows 7. The latest release is based on Firefox 153, the current ESR version. For users of old versions of Mac OS X, there are multiple options. Momiji is an actively maintained version of Firefox 140 ESR that runs on OS X 10.7 "Lion" and later. It continues the work of the Firefox Dynasty project, which we mentioned a year ago as an option for older Macs. Powerfox is similar: it targets Intel-based Macs and is based on the newer Firefox 153 ESR. The project also offers Powerfox Classic, which supports PowerMacs running all the way back to Mac OS X 10.3 "Panther." Aquafox is a modernized version of TenFourFox, for PowerMacs with Mac OS X 10.4. The SeaMonkey Project is a fork that continues development of the original pre-Firefox Mozilla Internet Suite: it's not just a web browser, but also contains an email/USENET client, an IRC client, an RSS reader, and a WYSIWYG HTML editor. It's still maintained, it's now 64-bit only, and it supports all major OSes – but compatibility may be an issue, as it uses a very old version of the Gecko rendering engine. We also found several Firefox forks that appear to be unmaintained and are probably best avoided. In addition to its ad blocker, Ghostery once offered a separate Ghostery Private Browser, but discontinued development in 2024. Similarly, the Mercury Browser for Windows and Linux was a more private Firefox with compiler optimizations so it ran faster. It never progressed beyond version 129, released a couple of years ago. The Cyberfox Browser was a 64-bit Windows version back when Mozilla didn't offer such things, although the project also offered a 32-bit version. It hasn't had a new release in eight years. Plasmafox was Firefox tweaked to better integrate with the KDE Plasma desktop on Arch Linux, but it fell off its perch after version 88.0.1, five years ago. These are the only ones of which the news has come to The Reg FOSS desk– although there may be many others. Anthropic decides to support OpenAI's markdown instructions spec
2026-09-18

Anthropic decides to support OpenAI's markdown instructions spec

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Devs cheer relief from compatibility headache Anthropic on Friday surprised the developer community by supporting rival OpenAI's mechanism for passing marching orders to AI agents. This makes life easier for folks who use both platforms. "We're adding support for AGENTS.md to Claude Code," said Claude Code engineer Thariq Shihipar in a social media post. "Starting today in version 2.1.277, if there is no CLAUDE.md in a folder, Claude will check for and use AGENTS.md." Claude Code users can toggle this behavior, he said, with the /config command. Until now, developers who use Claude Code alongside OpenAI Codex or other non-Anthropic tools have had to maintain two sets of Markdown instructions for their AI agents: CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md, not to mention project-specific versions of these files. These documents get read by coding agents with every request. They outline expected behavior, preferred tool usage, coding conventions, and so on. They lay the foundation of the agent's context. The two formats are similar but not identical – CLAUDE.md may contain Claude-specific instructions while AGENTS.md is intended to be tool agnostic. Because these documents can change, those working with AI agents have implemented various workarounds, like creating symlinks to keep CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md in sync. Last year, OpenAI contributed AGENTS.md to the Agentic AI Foundation, under the Linux Foundation, in a bid to build support for its standard. As of December 2025, more than 60,000 open source projects implemented AGENTS.md. The popularity of Claude Code has given Anthropic the latitude to insist on its own standards, but it appears the company has decided there's nothing to be gained from continued specification separatism. For those who use AI coding tools, this detente is an event of modest significance. Many widely followed software developers applauded the decision in social media posts. Thibault Sottiaux, OpenAI's head of core product, remarked, "Yay! This is the way. Come to the light," to which Shihipar responded with the handshake emoji (🤝). It's enough to bring a tear to the eye. ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy.
